TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth is likely to be in the hot seat after the midterm elections in November, if Democrats gain control of the House and possibly even the Senate, as many polls suggest. That legislative majority would give Democrats the power to convene oversight hearings and subpoena witnesses to testify about Hegseth’s prosecution of the war in Iran and handling of deadly strikes on alleged drug smugglers in the Caribbean. Some lawmakers even hope to impeach Hegseth for high crimes and misdemeanors. They accuse the onetime Fox News host of violating two major laws: the War Powers Resolution , which limits how long an unauthorized military conflict can last, and the War Crimes Act, which makes it a federal crime to violate certain international humanitarian laws. Republican Rep. Thomas Massie filed an impeachment resolution against Hegseth in September and is expected to force a vote on the matter when the House returns from its long midterms recess the week after the elections. In the case of the Iran war, the resolution accuses Hegseth of illegally continuing military strikes against Iran beyond the 60-day clock allowed under the law. He is further accused in the resolution of ordering the extrajudicial killing of over 220 alleged drug traffickers in the Caribbean Sea and Eastern Pacific, which even if the narcotics-smuggling charges were true is not grounds for summary execution under U.S. law. The secretary has also been criticized for overseeing secondary strikes on multiple survivors of the boat attacks who were still clinging to the wreckage of their destroyed vessels when they were killed. Additional matters that Democrats want to investigate include Hegseth’s role in the deadly Feb. 28 missile strike on the Minab elementary school in Iran, which killed over 150 people, including 120 children; his sharing in early 2025 of classified military operational details in a Signal group chat ; and his general noncompliance and stonewalling of congressional demands for military information that Congress is entitled

This talk about terminology reveals a fundamental misunderstanding of the threat. Over the past months, Russia’s pressure on Europe has changed in two ways. First, the attacks have become more frequent and moved toward the kinetic end of the spectrum: drones, explosives, sabotage of critical infrastructure. Second, Moscow has become visibly less careful about preserving plausible deniability, which has long been considered a defining feature of hybrid operations. On Sept. 1, Germany announced that Russia was behind a failed attempt to strike a Ukraine-bound cargo plane at Leipzig/Halle Airport with explosive-laden drones in August. Days later, a Russian frigate fired flares at a Danish military helicopter in the Baltic, and a drone likely carrying explosives was shot down over Lithuania. On Sept. 13, a Russian drone hit a train at Yahodyn, a village just on the Ukrainian side of the Polish border, minutes after another train carrying former European leaders—including former British Prime Minister Boris Johnson and former Swedish Prime Minister Carl Bildt—had pulled out. A strong case can be made that these actions were an openly aggressive answer to Berlin. The message to Germany and Europe is simple: Moscow does not care about being named. It wants Europe to know it is responsible, because it is betting that Europe’s response will only highlight its weakness. Two factors explain the shift.   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/22/europe-russia-hybrid-war-escalation/

* Could AI Disinformation Disrupt the Midterms?
  Foreign Policy | 22 Oct 10:00 UTC
  The answers are more encouraging than you might think.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Elections in the United States have been a battleground for foreign adversaries dating back at least a decade, with Russia, Iran, and China all using cyberattacks and social media campaigns to disrupt or interfere with the voting process. Add in the advanced cyber capabilities of the latest artificial intelligence models, their ability to generate bespoke information on command, and a highly polarized electorate, and the upcoming U.S. midterm elections begin to look like a potential minefield of misinformation. But the overarching message from more than half a dozen experts whom Foreign Policy spoke to, including current and former tech executives and government officials, is that it’s not time to press the panic button just yet. Elections in the United States have been a battleground for foreign adversaries dating back at least a decade, with Russia, Iran, and China all using cyberattacks and social media campaigns to disrupt or interfere with the voting process. Add in the advanced cyber capabilities of the latest artificial intelligence models, their ability to generate bespoke information on command, and a highly polarized electorate, and the upcoming U.S. midterm elections begin to look like a potential minefield of misinformation. But the overarching message from more than half a dozen experts whom Foreign Policy spoke to, including current and former tech executives and government officials, is that it’s not time to press the panic button just yet. “There are attempts to use AI to deceive, certainly, but they haven’t been particularly effective,” said Matt Masterson, a senior director for elections and societal resilience at Microsoft. “I haven’t seen anything that’s really taken hold in this election cycle or jumped out at me as really having an impact in that way.” That’s not to say that U.S. adversaries couldn’t be—or aren’t already—using AI tools to make their disinformation campaigns more effective. Large language models such as OpenAI’s ChatGPT, Anthropic’s Claude, and Google’s Gemini (to name only a few) give bad actors the ability to operate at a speed and scale that they previously couldn’t. “It’s helped them operationalize their work and almost industrialize it—it means that they can spin up content more quickly. They can also do a lot of research more effectively and quickly in terms of their target audience,” said Olga Belogolova, the director of the emerging technologies initiative at Johns Hop
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/22/midterm-election-ai-disinformation-openai-anthropic-microsoft/

* Experts: How are climate scientists using artificial intelligence?
  Carbon Brief | 22 Oct 10:45 UTC
  Artificial intelligence (AI) is developing at a rapid pace and is increasingly being used by scientists to better understand climate… The post Experts: How are climate scientists using artificial intelligence? appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: Artificial intelligence (AI) is developing at a rapid pace and is increasingly being used by scientists to better understand climate change. AI is perhaps best-known for its use in “large language models”, such as ChatGPT, Claude and Gemini. From weather forecasting to analysing satellite images, climate scientists are using various types of AI to develop faster and more efficient ways to study the warming planet. However, the technology comes with no shortage of risks and challenges, including overreliance on unverified results, intensive energy use and exacerbating existing inequalities in climate science. Carbon Brief spoke to 10 experts working across a range of climate-related fields – from developing climate forecasts to analysing changes in the energy grid – about how AI is being used. Their answers are set out below, grouped by theme. Dr Doug McNeall , science lead in data-driven climate modelling at the UK Met Office : One of the most significant recent developments has been the success of AI weather-prediction models , which learn from large volumes of weather data to predict the next few hours ahead and then repeatedly feed those predictions back into themselves to generate forecasts up to two weeks into the future. These models have demonstrated that AI can provide accurate predictions while requiring far less computing power than existing numerical models [which rely on mathematical equations to simulate the climate]. One of the main challenges is that AI weather-prediction models have been developed for relatively short timescales, where large amounts of observational data [for example, temperature, rainfall or sea ice extent data] is available and model performance can be evaluated quickly and rigorously. Climate prediction is fundamentally different. To provide useful climate information, we need to make projections for future climates that lie outside the observational record. One promising route forward is, therefore, to use AI to emulate or accelerate these existing models, rather than relying solely on purely data-driven approaches. This blended approach could make climate risk information far more accessible and affordable to produce, while retaining the physical understanding [based on mathematical and scientific principles] that underpins confidence in climate projections. Prof William Collins , professor of AI for climate science at the University of East Anglia : We’re seeing a really exciting renaissance in both weather and climat
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/experts-how-are-climate-scientists-using-artificial-intelligence

* SoFi Launches Tokenized Deposit That Earns 4.4% and Converts to a Stablecoin
  Decrypt | 22 Oct 12:01 UTC [also carried by: The Block, The Defiant]
  Each SOFIE token represents $1 on deposit at SoFi Bank, earning 4.40% APY with FDIC insurance—a yield-and-protection combination ordinary stablecoins lack.
  FULL TEXT: SoFi is giving its customers a way to earn bank-grade interest on digital dollars without giving up the speed of crypto, launching what it calls the first national bank-issued tokenized deposit for consumers. Announced Thursday, the product, SoFi Earn, trades under the ticker SOFIE, with each token representing $1 held on deposit at SoFi Bank. Holders earn 4.40% annual percentage yield, and the funds are FDIC-insured up to applicable limits, a key distinction from ordinary stablecoins. When members want to deploy the money, they can convert SOFIE one-to-one into SoFiUSD, the bank’s stablecoin , and then use it to buy crypto in the SoFi app or move dollars over blockchain rails around the clock. The pitch targets a specific gap. Stablecoins have become a popular way to park digital dollars, but many pay no interest and carry no deposit insurance. SoFi cited Federal Reserve research estimating that roughly $64 billion, or about 21% of the stablecoin market, sits idle in forgotten or inactive wallets. The company is pitching SOFIE as a way to keep that money earning until the moment it’s spent. “Too much money in the digital asset ecosystem is sitting idle,” CEO Anthony Noto said in a statement, arguing the product combines “the earning power and protections of a bank deposit with the speed and utility of digital assets.” SoFi Earn is the fifth crypto product the company has rolled out in under a year, part of an aggressive build-out of its digital-money ecosystem. SoFi returned to crypto trading in late 2025 after exiting the business years earlier, becoming the first nationally chartered U.S. bank to offer it to consumers, and members can now trade more than 40 tokens. The company followed that with SoFiUSD, the first stablecoin issued by a U.S. national bank , launched across Ethereum and Solana and now used to settle SoFi’s entire card program over Mastercard’s network. The move pushes SoFi deeper into a space where banks and fintech firms are racing to fold blockchain-based dollars into regulated products, following its earlier launch of business banking for digital assets and a stablecoin tie-up involving cryptocurrency exchange Kraken . By wrapping a yield-bearing, insured deposit in a token that moves like a stablecoin, SoFi is betting consumers want both at once. Start every day with the top news stories right now, plus original features, a podcast, videos and more.
  https://decrypt.co/381174/sofi-launches-tokenized-deposit-that-earns-4-4-and-moves-like-a-stablecoin

* A Saudi on the Silent Fear of the Houthi Strikes
  New Lines | 22 Oct 18:44 UTC
  As Houthi drones and missiles reach Riyadh and Dammam, a Saudi writer finds his compatriots anxious, distrustful of official accounts and unwilling to speak openly, while foreign residents bear the brunt of a hardening nationalism. The post A Saudi on the Silent Fear of the Houthi Strikes appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Amid the latest Houthi escalation against Saudi Arabia, drones and missiles have not been limited to southern cities and airports such as Abha and Najran, near the Yemeni border. They have also reached King Khalid International Airport in Riyadh and King Fahd International Airport in Dammam, causing widespread fear at home, alongside flight suspensions and temporary school closures. Yet this fear has more of a silent nature, one that leaves little trace on social media or in public discussions of any kind. We Saudis find ourselves in a unique position, different from that of the other parties involved. We have no voice that represents us under these circumstances, despite being directly affected by the war. I spoke with families, academics and others in the kingdom in recent days to inquire about what was happening on the ground and how it was being received by the population at large. Some responded merely with hints, signaling with their eyes and lips that I should not speak about the subject. One person I spoke to compared Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman with previous Saudi monarchs — like King Abdullah, King Fahd, King Khalid and King Faisal — under whose reign Saudis, despite general dissatisfaction, trusted their leaders and did not experience economic uncertainty. But that sense of stability has now faded, and concerns over domestic issues have intensified with the war, despite the crown prince’s reforms. Another told me that “we’ve gotten used to hearing Houthi missiles.” Perhaps those were not missiles but the sounds of drones. I don’t know, and I don’t think the person I spoke to cares about the difference. The young children in this person’s family are excited because school has been suspended, but that does not change the fact that there is a war going on, and that hearing drones and explosions has become normalized for many Saudis. What is even more troubling is that excessive nationalism has taken hold. Although Saudi Arabia is home to a large population of foreign workers and residents, whose numbers are close to those of Saudi citizens, the current crisis has also brought unfair treatment toward some foreigners. Many Saudis are demanding that foreigners, most of whom are in the country simply to earn a living, publicly express their opinions and show support for the kingdom. Kristin Idaszak | Longreads | October 22, 2026 | 3,741 words (13 minutes) The city of the dead is the obverse of the society of the living, or rather than the obverse, it is its image, its intemporal image. For the dead have gone through the moment of change, and their monuments are the visible sign of the permanence of their city. —Philippe Ariès, Western Attitudes Toward Death Shortly after I graduated from college, my best friend sent me a postcard from the Sedlec Ossuary, a Catholic chapel formally named the Cemetery Church of All Saints in Kutná Hora, a town about an hour east of Prague. Thanks to the plague, we have this work of totally weird art!” Immediately I started planning my own trip to Kutná Hora—a place made prosperous by silver mining but famous for its bone church—and arrived with the zeal of a pilgrim. Today the town has 20,000 residents, but in its 14th-century heyday it boasted almost four times as many souls. The late Middle Ages was a tough time to be alive, between the Black Death, various crusades and holy wars, and famine—not to mention the labor conditions of silver mining. Kutná Hora was also a popular place to die, and all the villagers wanted to spend eternity at the medieval Sedlec Abbey. After one of the crusades, an abbot had brought back a handful of soil, supposedly from Golgotha, and strewn it on the abbey grounds. Burial in that soil was believed to grant quicker access to heaven, which led to an overcrowded cemetery. The solution? Build a subterranean chapel to house the remains. And then a half-blind monk, in the wake of a terrible plague, exhumed the victims and used them to redecorate. An ossuary is any repository for human remains, but it’s also the term for a holy site that serves this function. Another name for this kind of church is charnel house, which to me evokes the image of an abattoir. Death historian Philippe Ariès connects the term to the French idiom une vieille carne , translated as an old nag or a tough piece of meat. * Big Tech’s thirst for water is sparking a global resistance
  Rest of World | 22 Oct 10:00 UTC
  This photograph taken on September 6, 2026 shows a placard opposing data centers displayed on a water dispenser during the People's Convention on AI Data Centers event at Visakhapatnam in India's Andhra Pradesh state. Construction of a massive Google data centre in the Indian city of Visakhapatnam, often called Vizag, has been met with re...
  FULL TEXT: After ChatGPT was released in November 2022, the tech industry was quick to herald the chatbot as the marker of a new wave of technological progress. As companies pushed to create more powerful AI models, the water demand of their ambitions took a while to become clear. In April 2023, a research paper out of the University of California estimated that training the large language model behind the initial version of ChatGPT had required 185,000 gallons of water. For every extended conversation someone had with the chatbot or every time they asked it to write an email, they were effectively pouring a bottle of water down the drain. That statistic got a lot of play when it was released. It became an early warning sign of what was coming as companies pushed chatbots as a universal technology that could replace psychologists, educators , lawyers, and even people’s friends or partners . As public concern grew, Google and OpenAI came out with their own figures. The companies suggested that querying one of their chatbots used mere drops of water. Independent researchers were not impressed, arguing the companies were taking a narrow view of the water use necessary to power and cool increasingly monstrous data centers. Those water demands can be quite significant too. A hyperscale data center can suck up between 1 and 5 million gallons of water a day, comparable to the amount used by a city of 10,000 to 50,000 people — depending on the part of the world we’re talking about. Consider how these companies often cluster data centers together and it becomes obvious what a challenge it can be to supply them. Data centers end up competing with communities over their potable water supply. The water demands of individual data centers are also continuing to grow. It’s not just that they need more water for cooling as they become larger and contain more servers. The graphics processing units (GPUs) that power generative AI are much more resource-intensive than the chips and storage devices that traditionally filled data center racks. GPUs run harder and throw off more heat , meaning they also need more energy or water to keep them cool. Data centers cannot just use any kind of water either; it has to be clean enough to ensure the equipment and pipes are not damaged. Tapping into public drinking water is both easier and cheaper than paying the cost of treatment themselves. That means data centers end up competing with communities over their potable water supply.   FULL TEXT: Earlier this year, I surfaced from sleep to a strange noise, a loud, human singing reverberating through our bedroom ceiling, then a low, almost animal groaning. My husband and I looked at each other. ‘I think Hatty’s in labour,’ he said. The rest of the day proceeded in the usual way, getting breakfast for our children, dropping them off at school, meetings, emails, booking in the car for an MOT, but these activities were interspersed with opening the door to midwives, making cups of tea for Hatty, and finding towels to drape around the birth pool that had appeared in our living room. Towards evening, our eighth housemate appeared in a rush of fluids. Two ambulance-loads of paramedics stood around like a flock of green and yellow London parakeets, blessedly unrequired. At one point, there were nine medical professionals, seven housemates, two grandparents of the new arrival and a cat in our terraced London house. It didn’t even feel that full, I thought later, as I poured bleach on bloodstained towels and binned a discarded catheter that had been overlooked in a corner. I am 42, and I did not expect to end up here. Like most of my peers, I was handed a vision of a home worth striving for with far, far fewer people in it and, to begin with, I followed that script. A middle-class 1980s child, I did well at school, moved away from my home county dormitory town for university, and never went back. London, jobs at the BBC, running a think tank all followed. I lived with good friends, and juggled seeing others between our clashing personal schedules. Then people began to pair up and procreate. I met someone, followed suit, and we clawed our way onto the very bottom of the housing ladder. It was a lovely life. A bit relentless, perhaps, a bit lonely around the edges, too, as the schedules got ever harder to tessellate, the work and care burdens heavier. But we had done it. Our little dyad, with dependants, was standing on its own feet, even if something felt decidedly unstable underneath. More space, more privacy, more control – all are sold as natural, unquestioned goods to be sought in the unconscious script that most of us carry. For the nuclear family, they are tokens of success and maturity, whereas forgoing these ‘goods’ and living with people you are neither shagging nor parenting has an association with low-status, studenty seasons. Not for proper grownups. My husband and I began to feel the strengthening drag of this story. I sometimes saw it in my mind

In total, it amounts to at least 16 languages. Only recently has anyone begun to think that it runs too long. “The many become one,” he said. “When I hear Mandarin or Farsi, these are not native languages to traditional Orthodox lands, but they are now.” There are few rooms on Earth where the same prayer is said in Arabic and Hebrew by people standing shoulder to shoulder. The languages spoken there predate most of today’s borders. Polina Sanfilippo, the choir director, was born in Moscow to a secular Russian Jewish family, moved to Israel at 7 and Canada at 16. She and her husband are godparents to a boy in the parish whose family is Egyptian. “Here I am, a Russian Jew, and my godson is Egyptian,” she said. “Where would that happen anywhere else?” What’s happening in that basement is happening, unevenly, everywhere Orthodoxy has landed across North America: The old model of one ethnicity, one language, one parish is buckling under the same pressures in different cities, and each community is answering differently. It was the undoing of a career on a meteoric rise, in a matter of 1,200 characters. Newsrooms across the political spectrum fed “It Was Either That or Die” into other AI detection sites, with conflicting results. For Pangram’s false positive rates, it should be noted, are not negligible. The company estimates that its overall false positive rate is 1 in 10,000, though other factors, including the language of the text, can affect that rate. Within days, the Goncourt Academy nonetheless struck the novel from its shortlist. The Orélien scandal, as it would come to be known, quickly took France by storm. Mainstream coverage focused obsessively on existential questions around the future of literature, with AI-detection tools held up as the now unavoidable sentinels of the (human-)written word. As a safety measure, the other 15 books shortlisted for the Goncourt were fed into Pangram as well. Craven is a dynamic counterexample to the men he writes about, less interested in bulking up the boundaries of his identity than testing their limits. * A Death That Divided America
  New Lines | 19 Oct 10:12 UTC
  Anand Giridharadas joins Faisal Al Yafai to discuss the death of Jordan Neely on a New York subway train, the abandonment felt by Americans everywhere and his new book, “Man in the Mirror.” The post A Death That Divided America appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Hosted by Faisal Al Yafai Featuring Anand Giridharadas Produced by Finbar Anderson Listen to and follow The Lede Apple Podcasts | Spotify | Youtube | Podbean Anand Giridharadas remembers the fallout from the May 2023 killing of a young homeless man on a New York city subway train. “From the moment that Daniel Penny put Jordan Neely in a chokehold and killed him on the floor of that F train in New York, it immediately was an American Rorschach test,” he tells Faisal Al Yafai on The Lede. “You were either looking at an act of heroism by a Marine who stood up to defend an endangered public, or you were looking at a white supremacist vigilante in Trump’s America.” The case was widely discussed across the United States, and the trial that followed the killing was followed closely by audiences on both sides of the political spectrum. In that subway car, Giridharadas saw “a microcosm of the country.” As such, he says, “the conditions in the country influence how that situation goes down. If you have appropriate levels of care for folks going through homelessness, people who are mentally ill, you probably wouldn’t have had that situation. … In other words, when the doors close, the country still is in that subway car.” Against the backdrop of this compelling allegory for modern America, Giridharadas, an acclaimed writer and former New York Times columnist, set out to write his recently published book, “Man in the Mirror: Hope, Struggle, and Belonging in an American City.” In it, he tells Al Yafai, he has endeavored to avoid pronouncing judgment on the killing itself. “I think this book will do something that’s actually much harder than making an argument. Meta has said that expanding Muse internationally would require support for local languages and integration with local services. AI agents would also need to understand the local culture , including the unique ways people communicate and bargain. “In many parts of the Global South, meaning is deeply contextual, shaped by family, community, religion, local institutions, and informal social networks,” Arora said. Without that understanding, agents might impose Western assumptions on their users, Usha Raman, an independent researcher on AI policy and digital culture, told Rest of World. “The danger then is that existing forms of cultural and economic hegemony continue and the rest of the world is forced to adapt,” she said. * Horrible, unmanly, indispensable
  Aeon | 19 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Renaissance Italians saw the gun as the Devil’s work. But soon they embraced this unchivalrous – and necessary – weapon - by Catherine Fletcher Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Detail from The Battle of Pavia, 1525 ( c 1529) by Robert Heller. Courtesy the Nationalmuseum, Sweden /Wikipedia If you were asked to think of a gun owner, then Lorenzo de’ Medici – the celebrated patron of Renaissance art – would probably not be the first person to come to mind. In fact, however, Lorenzo ‘the Magnificent’ owned five portable firearms, which are listed in his 1492 postmortem inventory. Guns and gunpowder were controversial. In art and literature, they were labelled the Devil’s work, and were perceived as fundamentally unchivalrous. Yet within half a century, by the early months of 1539, Lorenzo’s descendant Duke Cosimo de’ Medici had 91 firearms in his Florentine storerooms. Somehow, in the courts of late-Renaissance Italy, the gun had become an acceptable, even valued, weapon. Beyond the palazzi of the peninsula’s towns and cities, guns were also making inroads into more ordinary lives. There were no standing armies in the 16th-century Italian states. Their wars were fought through temporary mobilisations, and with considerable help from companies of mercenaries. Yet as the 1506 provision that established the Florentine militia made clear, ‘little hope’ could be placed ‘in foreign and hired arms’. Far better to establish a new magistracy with ‘full authority and power’ to enlist men from Florentine territory, at least 10,000 of them. One in 10 of these men were to be equipped with firearms, and 500 handguns ( schioppetti ) should be kept in the Palazzo della Signoria (the city hall, now known as the Palazzo Vecchio) along with ‘such money as should be necessary for the schioppetti ’, presumably to purchase gunpowder and shot. Three or four companies ( bandiere ) entirely of schioppettieri might also be established. This militia, most often noted for the role of Niccolò Machiavelli in its foundation, was to be organised after the fashion of the Germans, in whose cities there was a centuries-old tradition of civic defence and a duty on every male citizen to maintain suitable arms for the purpose. In fact, one of the Florentine militia’s early constables was German (a Giovanni Tedesco); another (Giliberto Spagnolo) came from Spain, a country making the running in the use of small arms on the battlefield. Giovanni Tedesco arranged target practice for his company, which from 1508 was extended more generally among the militia, with exercises taking place on a monthly basis. M ilitias were not new but, by the 1490s, large-scale foreign military 
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-renaissance-gun-from-diabolical-device-to-popular-militias

* How Shukri al-Quwatli’s Memoir Changes Our View of Syria’s History
  New Lines | 16 Oct 10:55 UTC
  Syria's presidents have been jailed, shot and exiled. Only one has left a memoir, and that has only recently been published.   FULL TEXT: On the slopes of Mount Qasioun, overlooking the Syrian capital in the Muhajireen neighborhood, is the presidential palace of Syria, built by the Ottoman governor Nazım Pasha between 1900 and 1905. Originally a two-floor private residence, it was the seat of power in Damascus briefly, during the reign of King Faisal (1918-1920), and then again, starting with President Shukri al-Quwatli, in 1943. Hafez al-Assad continued to use it until 1978, when he moved into the more quarantined Rawda Palace, judging Muhajireen as unsafe in light of his war with the Muslim Brotherhood. The old palace was abandoned and then placed at the disposal of the Republican Guard until 2010, when it was refurbished by Asma al-Assad, wife of Bashar al-Assad. On the night of the fall of Bashar’s regime, the palace was looted, only to be revamped again by Syria’s new President Ahmad al-Sharaa. And this is where he received Hala al-Quwatli, the last surviving child of the former president, Shukri al-Quwatli, on Oct. 9, 2026. Now in her mid-80s, Hala had not been to the palace since her father’s last term (1955-1958), and would probably never have returned if not for Assad’s ouster in December 2024. Al-Sharaa welcomed her on the second floor, facing the throne room that was created in 1920, on the occasion of the crowning of Faisal as king of Syria. She presented him with a copy of her father’s memoirs, published last April on the occasion of Syria’s 80th Independence Day celebration. From his deathbed in 1967, Quwatli bequeathed his memoirs to his two sons and three daughters. The documents were stored in Beirut, only to be partially damaged during the 1982 Israeli invasion of Lebanon and then moved to a bank in France, where they remained until mid-2025. Weeks after the fall of the Syrian regime, the family tasked me with compiling and editing the memoirs. The first seven chapters had already been written by his daughter Hana, who passed away in mid-2026. The rest were all recorded in Quwatli’s own voice, during his retirement between the years 1961 and 1963. They are full and complete, from his birth in 1891 until his meeting with British Prime Minister Winston Churchill in 1945, a turning point in the history of Syria that paved the way for it to join the United Nations. We then have one gap in 1946, when Syria got its independence from France, and another in 1958, when it merged with Egypt to form the United Arab Republic. * How to be a (Roman) man
  Aeon | 16 Oct 10:00 UTC [also carried by: Longreads]
  Cicero’s ideal of manly virtue stressed the performance of self-control and bravery, not boastful egotism and domination - by Catharine Edwards Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Roman bust, mid-1st century CE. Courtesy the Met Museum , New York What does it mean to be a man? In the early 21st century, masculinity is contested and fraught with anxiety; influencers offer models of manliness involving fast cars, sexual conquests, and building up muscle. Before the rise of feminism, they suggest, in some supposed Golden Age, everyone had a shared sense of what it meant to be a man – and being a man was unproblematically good. The Roman Empire, in their view, offers men today an inspirational example of power, hierarchy and achievement, which is why men think about it so much – unlike women, who are too immersed in their feelings… Manliness certainly was celebrated in the works of Cicero, republican Rome’s most eloquent statesman. In the mid-1st century BCE, his persuasive powers in the law courts and the senate were second to none; his speeches went on to inspire would-be orators well into the 20th century. He is often seen as the very embodiment of the Roman republic. The political world in which he flourished was a self-consciously masculine one; to be accused of acting like a woman was, for a Roman man, a grievous insult. Yet in Cicero’s writing we can find other strains too. For Roman men, anything labelled feminine was necessarily to be avoided, something to be ashamed of. But what exactly did it mean to act like a man? Ancient Rome in the 2nd and 1st centuries BCE saw the rise of a succession of military leaders who were promoted as masculine role models. The great general Marius won a series of victories in Spain, North Africa and on Italy’s northern frontier. For several years in a row, he was elected consul, one of Rome’s two chief executive officers. Unlike many other Roman politicians, he didn’t have an aristocratic background. One Roman writer imagines Marius fending off accusations that he was coarse in his manners: Here was a model of manliness forged on the battlefield. Given the military might of ancient Rome, it is surely unsurprising that Romans often associated masculinity with courage in battle. The word virtus , a term cognate with the Roman vir (meaning man as opposed to woman), clearly means ‘courage in war’ when it is first recorded in the mid-5th century BCE. Courage in battle could be taken as the foremost manifestation of Roman masculinity. The historian Sallust, writing in the 40s BCE, praises earlier generations of Romans for their pre-eminent manliness in war: ‘whenever their virtus repelled dangers, they
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-politics-of-masculinity-at-the-end-of-the-roman-republic

  Rest of World | 16 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Chinese open-weight AI models offer high capability at significantly lower costs than U.S. frontier systems, but face monetization and compute challenges, experts said at a Rest of World event in San Francisco.
  FULL TEXT: A slew of new open-weight artificial intelligence models from American and European companies aim to convert users of Chinese open models who are concerned about security, but fall short of the dominant Chinese systems on performance despite having greater resources, experts said at a Rest of World event in San Francisco. Last week, American startup Reflection launched its first open-weight model , and European labs Aleph Alpha and Mistral also released new open-weight models. Open models such as these offer users greater customization and control than the closed models of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Google at significantly lower costs. For businesses and governments in lower-income countries, in particular, cost is the primary factor, as well as the expertise needed to customize open models for their specific needs , including in areas such as education, healthcare, and farming, Daniel Björkegren, an assistant professor at Columbia University, said at the Rest of World event last week.
